# Repo code review, 2026-10-08

**Result:** Tests that run the real repo code prove 101 of the 102 findings. In 21 of those, part of the claim was wrong or the effect on real data is smaller. One finding (F39) is not reproduced. Of the 24 custom statistical methods, 23 give the same numbers as the standard versions on realistic inputs. 12 need fixes.

This is an agent review with agent-written tests. It is not an independent human review. Each test file is in `output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/`, so you can run it and read what it does.

**Fix status (same day):** 84 findings have a code fix and 17 design items stay open. See [2026-10-08_fix_log.md](2026-10-08_fix_log.md) for how each fix was checked, and [2026-10-08_design_items.md](2026-10-08_design_items.md) for the open decisions. The finding text below is unchanged from the review.

---

## Method

| Step | What happened |
|---|---|
| 1. Find | 17 agents read all 120 code files (about 26k lines). 2 of them traced layer, manifest and prompt rules across files. They reported 128 findings. After 4 were refuted and 22 duplicates were merged, 102 findings remain. |
| 2. Prove | For each finding, one agent wrote a test that imports and runs the real repo function. Rules: no copy of repo logic in the test; comments, docstrings and READMEs are not evidence; no network, API calls or real model weights. Generation findings use tiny random models built from the real cached configs and tokenizers. |
| 3. Audit | A second agent re-ran each test and checked: the verdict comes from real code output; the test checks the actual claim; the inputs are fair. One test failed the audit and was rewritten once. |
| 4. Re-run | I re-ran all 102 tests myself. All 102 gave the same result as the audit. (F9 timed out when run beside other tests. Alone, it took 3 minutes.) |
| 5. Standard methods | One agent listed 24 custom statistical or numerical methods. For each, one agent ran the repo function and a standard version on the same inputs, timed both, and recommended keep, fix, deduplicate or replace. |

**Test results:**

| Result | Count |
|---|---|
| ✅ Proven | 80 |
| ◐ Proven, narrower | 21 |
| ✗ Not reproduced | 1 (F39) |

**Limits:**

- Tests use synthetic data, tiny random models, and read-only loads of stored files. No test used real model weights or a GPU.
- Some findings say how much a bug changed a stored result. Only some tests measured that effect on the real data.
- Severity comes from the first review. Nobody re-rated it after the tests.
- F8 (MPS non-finite output) is proven only with a simulated NaN. The MPS trigger itself is unproven.

---

## Fix first

| # | Problem | What the test showed |
|---|---|---|
| F11 🔴 | `study_entropy.py` takes row numbers from the filtered 40-question table and uses them to read the full 240-question array. | 8 of 8 groups read rows from other roles or questions. Within-group variance came out up to 42.3 where the correct value is 0. |
| F1 🔴 | The batched path finds the response end with the tokenizer's end-of-text ID. The model stops on its own ID. | With the real cached Qwen2.5-3B base config and the Instruct tokenizer, 10 pad tokens were pooled. `prompt_avg` changed by up to 1.55 (72%). With matching IDs, the change was 0. The decoded text was the same, so the metadata hides it. OLMo and Tulu use the same ID for base and instruct. |
| F2 🟠 | Base models always get chat-template markup. There is no plain-text path. | The real CLI with the stored OLMo-7B base settings sent `<|system|>`, 3 `<|user|>` and 3 `<|assistant|>` tags to the base model. Without `--tokenizer`, it crashes. |
| F52 🟠 | The `manifold/` positive control uses the role-shuffle null. H1 is decided by `covgauss_null`. | On a planted curved manifold, `covgauss_null` gave p = 0.48. On a flat Gaussian it gave p = 0.55. The report still printed "PASS", then "H1 NOT SUPPORTED". |
| F67 🟠 | The steering hook layer is never checked against the cloud's layer or model. | A cloud from `hidden_states[17]` got a hook on `model.layers[18]` (`hidden_states[19]`), and the guard passed. A Qwen cloud ran on an OLMo model with no error. |
| F6 🟡 | The completed-run guard skips keys that old manifests lack. | A run with `--seed 1 --fewshot --batch_size 16` overwrote a copy of the 40q reference cloud. Only 7 of its 40 questions were the same. |
| F26 🟡 | `confound_variance` ignores the run's layer. | With `--layer 10`, the file named `_L10` held the layer-19 numbers. |
| F99 🟡 | The "current" robustness tier is a byte copy of the legacy tier. | All four files have the same hash. Fast mode printed "All invariants hold." |
| F101 ⚪ | `gate_check.py` G5 ignores sign. | `-0.66` passed against a source value of +0.656. |

**Correction to my earlier summary (F18):** `cos_axis` is exactly equal to `cos_centroid`, and `axis_proj` = `mean_norm` × `cos_centroid`. On the real data, after the ladder controls, `axis_proj` and `cos_centroid` still correlate at +0.785. So they overlap strongly but are not one measure.

**Findings that the tests weakened:**

- **F41:** the claimed harm is false. HDBSCAN recovers the groups with ARI 1.0. Only a parameter that does not match its comment remains.
- **F102:** the Q1 chance bias is real, but it is 1.7%, and no rule-2 verdict on the stored data changes.
- **F36:** the H0-entropy part is false. Entropy does not change under rescaling.
- **F5:** OLMo sampling is not unrestricted. The default `top_k=50` applies.
- **F39:** not reproduced. The real code raises an exception before NaN can reach the median.

---

## Custom methods: summary

Most custom code is the textbook method in a few lines, and it is often faster than the library. The problems are edge cases, copies that disagree, and a few wrong choices:

- **TPS kernel** (`manifold/tps.py:30`): it does not match the thin-plate kernel. Use `scipy.interpolate.RBFInterpolator`.
- **KDE density column:** it is a copy of the kNN-distance column (R² ≥ 0.99998 on 24 stored runs), so it adds no information.
- **Entropy shuffle null** (`study_entropy.py`, `figures_entropy.py`): it uses a per-target 95th percentile, which has no correction for 27 targets. The ladder copy is correct (Westfall–Young max-T).
- **Copies that disagree:** participation ratio has 8 copies, the assistant axis has 7, and the ID wrappers use MLE k = 10 in some copies and k = 20 in others.
- **`bh_fdr`:** it is correct, but `scipy.stats.false_discovery_control` does the same job.
- **Keep:** partial correlation (matches statsmodels to 1e-14 and is 4–11× faster than the libraries), Holm, kappa, CKA, mKNN, persistence, Ricci curvature, the two-way decomposition, and the smoothing spline (scipy cannot fit one shared λ across 2048 dimensions).

#### partial-correlation: fix

- **Repo code:** exploratory/per_persona/stats_utils.py:35 (partial_corr, k=1), exploratory/per_persona/stats_utils.py:55 (partial_corr_multi), robustness/legacy/check_artifacts.py:51 (partial_corr_multi copy, r only, no p, no NaN guard), callers: study_ladder.py:169-241, study_entropy.py:389,420, id_vs_axis.py:132-141, confound_sysprompt.py:211-213, compare_stages.py:44, study_entropy_fallback.py:156, robustness/current/calibrate_tolerances.py:148
- **Standard:** pingouin.partial_corr(data, x, y, covar=[...], method='pearson') (stdvenv); statsmodels OLS residuals + scipy.stats.pearsonr; formula t = r*sqrt((n-k-2)/(1-r^2)), df = n-k-2
- **Agreement:** I tested 156 cases. Each one called the real stats_utils.partial_corr, stats_utils.partial_corr_multi and robustness/legacy/check_artifacts.partial_corr_multi. I compared them with pingouin 0.6.1 partial_corr and with statsmodels OLS residuals plus scipy pearsonr, using the formula t = r*sqrt((n-k-2)/(1-r^2)) with df = n-k-2. VALID INPUTS (max abs diff / max rel diff): - Synthetic n=275, k=1: r 1.8e-15 / 1.1e-14 against statsmodels and 8.0e-13 against pingouin. p 3.6e-15 / 3.7e-13 against statsmodels and 6.0e-13 against pingouin. - Synthetic n=275, k=2: r 2.9e-15 against statsmodels and 1.3e-11 against pingouin. p 1.8e-14 against statsmodels and 1.4e-11 against pingouin. - Synthetic n=275, …
- **Speed:** Timings are per call on CPU with n=275. - k=2: the repo partial_corr_multi takes 385 us. The legacy copy takes 279 us (it has no p). statsmodels OLS plus pearsonr takes 1488 us. pingouin.partial_corr takes 4363 us. - k=11: the repo takes 987 us, the legacy copy 1005 us, statsmodels 2091 us and pingouin 8199 us. - k=0: the repo takes 145 us. - The repo is about 4x faster than statsmodels and 11x …
- **Complexity:** The method is not more complicated than the problem needs. It is the textbook method: Frisch-Waugh-Lovell residualisation by lstsq, Pearson r on the residuals, and a t-test with df = n-k-2 (Fisher 1924; Kim 2015, ppcor). It is about 15 lines. It is simpler and faster than pingouin, and it is more stable than pingouin, which inverts a covariance matrix and gave a wrong r on the perfect-collinearity case. The problems are elsewhere: (a) Duplication. partial_corr (k=1) is a strict subset of …
- **Reason:** On valid inputs the repo estimator is correct. On realistic inputs (n=275, k=1-11, and the stored L19 panel) it matches statsmodels to about 1e-14 and pingouin to about 1e-11, for both r and p. It is also 4-11x faster, so replacing it with a library would add cost and gain nothing. Fix the edge-case behaviour instead: (1) In partial_corr_multi, return p = NaN, not 0.0, when r is not finite or when n-k-2 <= 0. The repo's own partial_corr already returns NaN when r is NaN. (2) Return NaN when the residual variance of x or y is near zero relative to the input variance, or reject a predictor or target that is a column of Z. study_entropy.build_ladder regresses mean_tokens and trunc_rate on themselves in the 'ctrl_all' rung. The real caller then reports a planted r = 0.40 as r = 0.016, p = 0.80. Those meaningless rows also go into q_global. (3) Optionally, use the rank of [1, Z] for k, and …

#### tps-manifold-r2: fix

- **Repo code:** manifold/tps.py:30 (thin_plate_kernel r^(dim-2) log r), manifold/tps.py:63 (_fit augmented linear solve), manifold/tps.py:97 (project, forward-difference Gauss-Newton), manifold/tps.py:147 (reconstruction), manifold/pipeline.py:69 (fit_manifold: control points = PCA(anchors,k)), manifold/pipeline.py:194 (pca_plane_r2 baseline), exploratory/per_persona/metrics.py:116 (_curvature: spline_r2, curvature_gain; no longer in panel)
- **Standard:** scipy.interpolate.RBFInterpolator(control, targets, kernel='thin_plate_spline', degree=1, smoothing=0); projection via scipy.optimize.least_squares; polyharmonic splines (Duchon 1977; Wendland 2005): phi = r^(2m-k) for odd k, r^(2m-k) log r for even k
- **Agreement:** All checks call the real repo code (manifold.tps, manifold.pipeline, metrics._curvature). Data: the real response-token role cloud (6900 x 50, 276 role means, layer 19, read-only), a planted curved 3-D manifold, and synthetic edge cases. KERNEL (tps.py:30), the root mismatch. The code computes r^(dim-2) log r, so the result is not the thin-plate spline at any dim: - dim=2 gives log r, not r^2 log r (the docstring says r^2 log r, but the code returns -2.30 at r=0.1, which is log r). log r is the harmonic kernel. It is not conditionally positive definite here: the null(P^T) block has 236 positive and 37 negative eigenvalues. The code sets the kernel to 0 at r<1e-10, so the surface has a jump …
- **Speed:** The test machine runs on CPU only. Fit, 276 anchors: - repo fit_manifold (PCA + solve): 0.49-0.58 s - scipy RBFInterpolator fit: 0.08-0.31 s Projection, real cloud: - repo vectorised GN on all 6900 points: 1.4 s (k=2) and 1.6 s (k=3). - scipy least_squares in a per-point loop: 1.84 s per 150 points with 1 start, which extrapolates to about 85 s for 6900 points. With 5 starts it takes 18 s per 150 …
- **Complexity:** There are two parts. 1. The custom surface (thin_plate_kernel + _fit + decode) adds nothing over scipy.interpolate.RBFInterpolator(kernel='thin_plate_spline' or 'linear', degree=1, smoothing=0). That one call does the same augmented solve, with internal polynomial scaling and a singularity check. The hand-written version also has a wrong kernel exponent (r^(dim-2) log r instead of the polyharmonic r^(2m-d) [log r]). At k=2 this gives a jump at every anchor, and at k=3 it gives a non-standard r …
- **Reason:** Recommendation: FIX the kernel. Use scipy RBFInterpolator(kernel='thin_plate_spline' or 'linear', degree=1) in place of the custom kernel and solve, keep the fast GN projector, and add a nearest-anchor baseline. The repo kernel at manifold/tps.py:30 is r^(dim-2) log r, which is not the thin-plate/polyharmonic kernel: - k=2 gives log r, which is not conditionally positive definite here. The surface jumps by 70 units within 1e-9 of each anchor, so the run.py k=2 robustness row is in practice nearest-anchor R2. - k=3 gives r log r instead of r. It is 20-72% off the scipy standards on the real anchors, it reconstructs a planted curved manifold 40-60% worse, and it shifts the decider R2 by up to 0.03 on a subset. The parts that are correct and should stay: - The projector matches least_squares and is about 50x faster. Keep it and point it at the scipy surface. - reconstruction R2 matches …

Valid inputs: - Max abs diff was at most 2.2e-16 and max rel diff was at most 2.3e-16 (rounding only) in every case below. - Realistic inputs were p-values made by the repo's own partial_corr_multi on 275 synthetic roles with 2 controls, at 6/12/20/54/120/300 tests. - Other cases: 2000 uniform null p-values, p-values down to 1e-300, heavy ties (rounded to 2 decimals), all p equal to 0.03, exact 0 and 1 values, all zeros, all ones, n=1, n=2, input in descending order, and empty input. - The count with q<0.05 was identical in …
- **Speed:** Mean time per call, repo bh_fdr vs scipy false_discovery_control, on CPU: | Number of tests | Repo | scipy | |---|---|---| | 20 | 0.018 ms | 0.051 ms | | 300 | 0.24 ms | 0.06 ms | | 2000 | 1.75 ms | 0.14 ms | | 100,000 | 96 ms | 9.3 ms | The repo version is O(n log n), but its step-up loop runs in Python, so it is about 10x slower at large n. The callers run 6 to about 300 tests, so the cost is …
- **Complexity:** The function is 8 lines and implements the textbook BH step-up correctly: Benjamini and Hochberg (1995), q_(i) = min over j>=i of p_(j)*m/j, capped at 1. Starting the loop with prev=1.0 is what applies the cap. It is not more complicated than the problem needs. But it adds nothing over scipy.stats.false_discovery_control, which is already a dependency of stats_utils. The two differ only in input checks. scipy raises an error on NaN or p outside [0,1]. The repo silently turns NaN into q=1 and …
- **Reason:** bh_fdr is correct. On every valid input, including the realistic p-values made by the repo's own code, it matches scipy and statsmodels to 2e-16. The q<0.05 decisions are identical in every case. So no BH q-value reported from these four scripts is wrong because of this function. Even so, I recommend replacing its body with scipy.stats.false_discovery_control(p, method='bh'). scipy is already imported in stats_utils. - Real use: it gives the same numbers on every valid input, so old results stay reproducible. - Errors: NaN or out-of-range p would raise an error instead of becoming q=1 (which also inflates the other q-values) or a negative q. - Maintenance: it removes hand-written statistics code. If a caller ever has NaN p-values on purpose, it should drop them before the correction. It should not depend on the repo's current silent NaN-to-1 rule. Separate low-priority note: …

Both give exactly the same values (max abs diff 0.0, max rel diff 0.0, zero reject@0.05 disagreements) in these cases: a run.py-like set of 5 tests; a sweep-like set of 8 median permutation p-values; uniform, permutation-style ((1+k)/(1+n_perm)) and mixed-signal sets at m=20, 100 and 300; ties; all-equal p; exact 0 and 1 values; m=1; m=2; empty dict; denormal or tiny p (5e-324, 1e-300). - **Speed:** Mean time per call, repo holm vs statsmodels multipletests: m=8: 22 us vs ~30 ms; m=300: 0.43 ms vs ~23 ms; m=10000: 17 ms vs 33 ms. The statsmodels per-call figures look inflated by overhead in this env, but both are negligible. The callers use m of about 5 (run.py) and about 6-10 (sweep.py). - **Complexity:** No. The function has 7 lines and is the textbook Holm (1979) step-down: sort ascending, multiply by (m-i), take the running max, clip to 1. It adds nothing beyond the standard method. Its only differences are dict-in/dict-out handling and no NaN handling. There is one copy. I confirmed at run time that manifold.run.holm and manifold.sweep.holm are the same object as runlog.holm, so no duplication exists. statsmodels is not in the project venv. The NaN behaviour is wrong but has no effect now: NaN becomes 1.0 and poisons later p-values in an order-dependent way, but the current callers cannot produce NaN p. A cheap guard is optional: raise on NaN, or pass NaN through. The real NaN risk is upstream. In manifold/pipeline.py:322, separation_stats maps a NaN R² to the minimum p. The median-of-seeds input in sweep.py:268 should be labelled descriptive. Neither issue is a defect in holm.

The differences are close to machine precision in the lambda range that is used. The GCV grid picks a lambda within a factor of 1.33 of the true optimum, with GCV excess 6.5e-4 or less. The custom code is justified because scipy cannot fit a shared-lambda vector spline, and the repo code is fast enough. Two low-cost fixes would improve the code. These are not reasons to replace it: (a) Record or warn when fit_gcv picks a grid-edge lambda. Lambda scales as x^3, and a run with x scaled by 100 silently returned 1e6 when the optimum was 6e6. (b) Pass the tie counts as weights after the tie averaging, or document the change. The current result differs from the raw-data penalised fit by 3.9% when ties occur. This test did not check the real centroid cloud. It also did not …

#### semantic-entropy: keep

- **Repo code:** exploratory/per_persona/entropy.py:183 (cluster_greedy), exploratory/per_persona/entropy.py:207 (discrete_entropy), exploratory/per_persona/study_entropy_fallback.py:38 (cluster_by_sim), exploratory/per_persona/study_entropy.py:528-530 (SE_centred, E_role)
- **Standard:** Farquhar et al. 2024 Nature reference code (github.com/jlko/semantic_uncertainty: get_semantic_ids, cluster_assignment_entropy); scipy.stats.entropy(counts)
- **Agreement:** All tests call the real repo functions: E.cluster_greedy, E.discrete_entropy, S.score_groups, FB.cluster_by_sim, and the real S.main() up to per_role_entropy_L19.csv. I mocked only data loading, the NLI model, the tokenizer and control_pairs. (1) discrete_entropy vs scipy.stats.entropy(counts) and the jlko cluster_assignment_entropy: max abs diff 0.0 on every partition. That covers the exhaustive graphs, 4 x 11,040 realistic groups, M=1, M=2, all-split, one-cluster and M=40. (2) cluster_greedy vs the published pseudo-code (Kuhn et al. 2023 Alg. 1 / Farquhar ED Fig. 1, "join the first matching class"). I enumerated every symmetric equivalence graph for M=2..6 (33,866 graphs). Partition …
- **Speed:** On 11,040 groups (the real study size), the real S.score_groups took 0.30-0.56 s. The jlko reference took 0.74-1.25 s. scipy.stats.entropy called per group took 4.8-5.3 s, from about 0.5 ms of overhead per call. For 100k calls, cluster_greedy + discrete_entropy took 3.0 s and scipy.stats.entropy alone took 51.8 s. The centring is one pandas groupby (well under 1 s). statsmodels two-way FE took …
- **Complexity:** The code is not more complicated than the problem needs. - cluster_greedy is a 10-line form of the paper's Algorithm 1 and agrees with it on all 33,866 enumerated graphs. - discrete_entropy is the standard plug-in Shannon entropy, identical to scipy.stats.entropy and about 17x faster per call. - The per-question centring is the simplest standard adjustment. It equals two-way fixed effects exactly on balanced data and to 1e-4 on the mildly unbalanced data the study has, so it is a sound simple …
- **Reason:** Running the real repo code shows the three parts are correct: - discrete_entropy equals scipy.stats.entropy exactly. - cluster_greedy reproduces the published Farquhar/Kuhn greedy algorithm exactly. - The per-question centring matches two-way fixed-effects OLS to 1e-13 on balanced data and 1e-4 on unbalanced data. All three are simple and fast, and the two clustering copies agree. The one real difference from the jlko reference code (first-match vs last-match membership) appears only when the NLI relation is not transitive. There it moves single-group SE by up to 0.17 nats and per-role means by up to 0.04 nats (r = 0.99). Report this as an order and transitivity sensitivity, not as a bug. Optional clean-ups: - Replace cluster_by_sim with cluster_greedy(sim >= tau). - Add guards for empty input and an m_total mismatch. - Run an order-free connected-components variant as a sensitivity …

#### cohen-kappa: keep

- **Repo code:** exploratory/per_persona/study_entropy.py:302 (cohen_kappa, binary, hand-written), steering/validate_judge.py:213 (compute_kappa, wraps sklearn cohen_kappa_score with pinned labels), exploratory/per_persona/study_entropy_fallback.py:92 (uses S.cohen_kappa to choose tau)
- **Standard:** sklearn.metrics.cohen_kappa_score; Cohen 1960
- **Agreement:** I imported and ran the real repo code. I compared it with sklearn 1.9.0 cohen_kappa_score in the project venv and with statsmodels 0.15.0 cohens_kappa in stdvenv. I also wrote the Cohen (1960) Eq. 1 formula, p_e = sum_k p_k. * p_.k, as a third reference. (1) Binary study_entropy.cohen_kappa, 16 cases: n = 20, 100, 400, 2000, with rater prevalence from 0.1 to 0.9 and agreement from 0.55 to 0.9. Max abs diff vs sklearn is 2.2e-16. Max rel diff is 4.8e-16. Max abs diff vs statsmodels is also at 1e-16 level. (2) Real fallback tau scan. I ran study_entropy_fallback.main() with mocked load_tier, build_groups and EmbedPredicate. The input was 60 groups x 10 answers and 400 judge pairs. I made …
- **Speed:** I measured on CPU with mean over repeats: - n=400 (judge-pair size): repo 0.065 ms, sklearn 1.68 ms. - n=20,000 (A4 pair size): repo 0.17 ms, sklearn 5.2 ms. - n=1,000,000: repo 10 ms, sklearn 174 ms. The repo is about 17-26x faster, but both times are negligible. The 50-tau fallback scan needs about 3 ms in the repo and 85 ms with sklearn. Most of that scan's time goes to the pandas iterrows …
- **Complexity:** No. The binary cohen_kappa has 4 lines and uses the textbook 2x2 formula: p_o = mean(a==b) and p_e = pa*pb + (1-pa)*(1-pb). It returns NaN when p_e = 1. It gives the same values as sklearn and statsmodels to machine precision. It is simpler than the library call, so it adds no complexity. What it lacks: - It does not check its input. np.asarray(x, bool) binarises any value without a warning, makes NaN True, and lets a length-1 array broadcast. - It does not catch empty input. These gaps do not …
- **Reason:** The hand-written binary kappa equals sklearn, statsmodels and the Cohen (1960) formula to within 3e-16 on all realistic cases. This includes the real fallback tau scan run through main(), which picks the same tau* as sklearn. validate_judge.compute_kappa is already sklearn and also agrees with both other references. The binary function is shorter and faster than the library call, and all three call sites use the same code, so there is no copy to deduplicate. The divergences occur only on inputs that the current callers never produce: NaN becomes True, ints are binarised and length-1 arrays broadcast, all with no warning. These do not justify a replacement. A one-line shape/dtype assert would be optional hardening. One real issue is in a caller, not in the kappa function. The A4 pair_kappa at study_entropy.py:580 includes the always-False diagonal of the NLI matrices. This raised kappa …

- **Standard:** statsmodels.stats.outliers_influence.variance_inflation_factor (with a constant column); sklearn cross_val_predict + r2_score; Hastie, Tibshirani & Friedman, ESL sec 7.10

That covers the real instruct and base L19 panels (M0, M1 and full model), a synthetic n=275 p=21 collinear set, n=275 p=300, simplex, duplicated rows, a constant column, n=6, p=1 and pure noise. M0 was compared with DummyRegressor. - Compared with an independent numpy ridge, the max abs prediction diff …
- **Speed:** Timings are on the real n=275, p=24 panel, CPU, median of 5 runs. - Repo cv_r2: 0.354 s in total. The sklearn cross_val_predict part takes 0.118 s. The Python bootstrap loop (2000 draws) takes about 0.236 s. A vectorised scipy.stats.bootstrap with 2000 draws takes 0.104 s. - Repo vif (p=24): 0.176 s. diag(inv(corr)) takes 0.0004 s, about 480x faster. - Neither function has a complexity problem at …
- **Complexity:** The code is not more complex than the problem needs. - cv_r2 is a thin wrapper around the standard sklearn recipe: Pipeline(StandardScaler, RidgeCV) in cross_val_predict with KFold(5, shuffle, seed 0), then a pooled R2. This is the ESL 7.10 approach, with alpha chosen inside each training fold. - cv_r2 adds two things to the standard method: 1. A manual intercept-only branch. It is equivalent to DummyRegressor and matches it exactly. 2. A 25-line hand-written percentile bootstrap. …
- **Reason:** Both functions give the same numbers as the standard tools, to machine precision, on the real per-role panels and on synthetic inputs. The standard tools are sklearn cross_val_predict + r2_score, an independent ESL-style ridge with LOO alpha selection, and statsmodels VIF with a constant. The bootstrap CI matches scipy.stats.bootstrap within Monte Carlo error. The edge cases that differ (constant y, zero-variance column, n<5) cannot occur in main(). It drops NaN rows and zero-variance columns, and it runs only at n=275. The code already follows the standard method, so nothing needs replacing. Two optional small changes are possible. Neither changes any reported number. - Swap the manual bootstrap loop for scipy.stats.bootstrap. It is shorter and about 2x faster. - Guard ss_tot==0 in cv_r2. The CI does not include refit variance. Any write-up should say so, because the CI is slightly …

## All findings by area

102 unique findings after merging 22 duplicates. "Test result" comes from running the real code. "Proven, narrower" means the real code shows the main defect, but part of the claim was wrong or the effect on real data is smaller; the details say which part. Severity is from the first review and was not re-rated.

## Details

In F-number order. "Test result" is the auditor's conclusion after re-running the test. "Test output" is what the test printed.


### F1. Batched path pools trailing pad tokens when model eos != tokenizer eos

✅ proven · 🔴 critical · [src/manifold_persona/generate.py:134](../../src/manifold_persona/generate.py#L134)

- **Claim:** generate_and_extract_batched finds the response end by searching for tokenizer.eos_token_id, but model.generate stops on model.generation_config.eos_token_id. When generate_and_extract_roles.py swaps in another repo's tokenizer (--tokenizer, line 263), those ids differ. The search then misses, so r becomes the full batch length, and the stop token plus every trailing pad is pooled into prompt_avg and becomes prompt_last.
- **Failure case:** Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B base run with --tokenizer Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct --batch_size 16. Base generation stops on 151643 (<|endoftext|>), which is also pad_id. The search looks for 151645 (<|im_end|>) and never finds it. Every finished response is padded to the batch maximum (~128 tokens), and those pads go into the mean. The decoded text uses skip_special_tokens, so the pads do not show in metadata.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I re-ran the test. The real generate_and_extract_batched received a tokenizer with eos 151645 and a model with eos 151643. It did not find 151645, so it pooled the model eos and 10 trailing pad tokens into sample 0. Compared with the real unbatched path, prompt_avg differed by maxabs 1.55 (rel 0.72) and prompt_last by 0.98. With the eos aligned and identical generated tokens, the control differed by 0. The decoded text was the same in both runs, so the metadata does not show the pads.
- **Test output:** tokenizer eos_token_id=151645 pad=151643; model generation_config eos_token_id=151643. Sample 0 generated [137554, 151643, then 10 more 151643 pads]: the true length through eos is 2 and 10 trailing pads follow; 151645 does not appear. Sample 0 prompt_avg: control (eos aligned) vs unbatched maxabs=0; bug vs unbatched maxabs=1.549, rel=0.72. prompt_last: control maxabs=0, bug maxabs=0.977. Sample 1 (no eos) is unaffected (diff 0). Decoded responses are identical in the bug and control runs ('между'). Reproduced with seeds 0, 1 and 2.
- **Test file:** [repro/F1_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F1_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F2. Base models can only be fed chat-template markup, never plain text

✅ proven · 🟠 high · [src/manifold_persona/generate.py:141](../../src/manifold_persona/generate.py#L141)

- **Claim:** Both generate_and_extract and generate_and_extract_batched always render input with tokenizer.apply_chat_template(..., add_generation_prompt=True), and there is no plain-text path. The prompt extractor has the same problem through prompts_roles.render_prompts. A base checkpoint has no chat template, so the caller swaps in the instruct tokenizer (--tokenizer). The base model then reads <|system|>/<|user|>/<|assistant|> role markup it was never trained on. This breaks the project rule that base models get plain text only.
- **Failure case:** output/olmo7b_hf_inputs_clean/compact/base and output/_hf_stage/tulu3_compact/base were built from allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B and NousResearch/Meta-Llama-3.1-8B. Their manifests have tokenizer_name=<instruct tokenizer>, fewshot=true and stop_marker='<|user|>'. Each base point is the response to an out-of-distribution chat scaffold, not to a plain-text role prompt. The base manifest shows share_looping=0.21, against <0.001 for the aligned stages. Any base-vs-aligned geometry difference is confounded with the scaffold.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I re-ran the test and it printed BUG_REPRODUCED. The real generate_and_extract, generate_and_extract_batched, render_prompts and the generate_and_extract_roles CLI all raise ValueError with the OLMo-2 base tokenizer, because it has no chat template. With the stored manifest config (instruct tokenizer, fewshot), a spy on generate captured what the base model receives: <|system|>/<|user|>/<|assistant|> chat markup. These results come from running the real repo code, not from a copy or a comment.
- **Test output:** base_tok.chat_template is None -> True. generate_and_extract(base_tok) and generate_and_extract_batched(base_tok) raise "ValueError: Cannot use chat template functions because tokenizer.chat_template is not set". render_prompts(base_tok) raises the same ValueError. CLI B1 (stored base config) status ok. Manifest: model_name=allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B, tokenizer_name=allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct, fewshot=True, stop_marker='<|user|>'. First input to the base model: '<|endoftext|><|system|>\nYou are an aberration...\n<|user|>\nWhat causes the seasons on …
- **Test file:** [repro/F2_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F2_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F3. Default-role prompt text differs per stage via {model_name}

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [src/manifold_persona/prompts_roles.py:71](../../src/manifold_persona/prompts_roles.py#L71)

- **Claim:** build_role_records replaces {model_name} with model_display. Both extractors set model_display to args.model.split('/')[-1], so each stage writes a different system prompt into the 'default' role, which anchors the Assistant axis. The --tokenizer help text says cross-stage runs see identical token sequences. That claim is false for 20% of the default-role rows.
- **Failure case:** In the OLMo-2-7B lineage, default instruction_idx=3 reads 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B.' (base), '...-SFT.' (sft), '...-DPO.' (dpo) and '...-Instruct.' (rlvr). That is 40 of 200 default rows per stage. The axis is mean(default) - mean(all), so per-stage axis, projection and cosine comparisons mix a real stage effect with a prompt-text change. Pretraining-revision runs of one repo all get the same name, so the confound appears only between repos.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I re-ran the real generate_and_extract_roles.py and build_and_extract_roles.py for the 4 OLMo-2-7B stages, with the same instruct tokenizer for all. In both scripts, 40 of the 200 default-role rows (20%) have different system text and different token ids per stage, for example 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B-SFT.'. Stored run metadata in output/steer_olmo and output/olmo7b_backups shows the same per-stage names in the real runs.
- **Test output:** generate_and_extract_roles: base 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B.', sft 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B-SFT.', dpo 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B-DPO.', rlvr 'You are OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct.'. Each string is on 40 rows. The default rows whose token ids differ across stages with the same --tokenizer: 40/200 = 20% (expected 40/200). Rows that differ across all roles: 40/55200. build_and_extract_roles: the same 4 strings, with 40/200 default rendered texts that differ. Stored metadata (tokenizer_name=allenai/OLMo-2-1124-7B-Instruct for all): base, sft and rlvr each hold their own name string on 40/200 default rows.
- **Test file:** [repro/F3_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F3_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F4. --stop-marker is silently ignored on the default batch_size=1 path

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [src/manifold_persona/generate.py:26](../../src/manifold_persona/generate.py#L26)

- **Claim:** Only generate_and_extract_batched accepts stop_marker. The sequential generate_and_extract, which runs when batch_size=1 (the CLI default), has no stop_marker parameter. The caller drops the argument there but still writes stop_marker into ckpt_config and the final manifest.
- **Failure case:** A user runs a base model with `--stop-marker '<|user|>' --fewshot` and the default --batch_size 1, for example a local MPS run or a pilot. The base model never emits eos, so each response runs to max_new_tokens and includes invented '<|user|> ... <|assistant|>' turns. Those tokens are pooled into resp_avg, and resp_last becomes a token inside the invented turn. The manifest says stop_marker='<|user|>', so no reader can tell the cut was not applied. The batched and unbatched clouds of the same stage then differ for a reason that is not recorded.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. In extraction/generate_and_extract_roles.py lines 278-281, the batch_size=1 branch calls generate_and_extract without stop_marker, and that function has no such parameter. The re-run shows these effects: the bs=1 responses keep the invented turn, the manifest says stop_marker='<|user|>', and resp_last differs from the bs=2 output by up to 2.94. The --help text says the flag works only on the batched path, but nothing warns at run time and the manifest record is wrong.
- **Test output:** With batch_size=1, the manifest has stop_marker='<|user|>' and batch_size=1. response[0] is 'The answer is simple.\n<|user|>\nInvented follow-up question?\n<|assistant|>\nInvented answer that the base model made up.' With batch_size=2, the manifest has stop_marker='<|user|>' and response[0] is 'The answer is simple.\n'. resp_last max |bs1 - bs2| = 2.94. Every bs1 response has the invented turn. No bs2 response has it.
- **Test file:** [repro/F4_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F4_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F5. Hidden generation_config settings change 'greedy' decoding per model

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [src/manifold_persona/generate.py:135](../../src/manifold_persona/generate.py#L135)

- **Claim:** gen_kwargs set only max_new_tokens, do_sample, pad_token_id and (when sampling) temperature. model.generate merges these with the model's generation_config.json. Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, the default MODEL_NAME, ships repetition_penalty=1.05, top_k=20 and top_p=0.8. Llama-3.1 and Tulu ship top_p=0.9. OLMo-2 and Qwen2.5-3B base ship none of these. No manifest records the merged settings.
- **Failure case:** Reference Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct clouds recorded as do_sample=False are greedy plus a 1.05 repetition penalty. A Qwen2.5-3B base run under the same flags is pure greedy. A base-vs-instruct comparison therefore changes the decoding rule as well as the weights. With --do_sample, 'temperature 1.0' means top-k 20 / top-p 0.8 for Qwen, top-p 0.9 for Llama/Tulu, and unrestricted sampling for OLMo.
- **Test result:** The finding is true, apart from one wrong detail about OLMo. I re-ran the test (about 12 s). Both real repo generators send only max_new_tokens/do_sample/pad_token_id/temperature to model.generate. With Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct's generation_config, a do_sample=False run gets RepetitionPenalty 1.05, and sampling also gets top_k=20 and top_p=0.8. Qwen base and OLMo greedy runs get no processors. Tulu/Llama sampling adds top_p=0.9. The manifest.json and provenance.json that the real script writes record none of these settings. The claim that OLMo sampling is 'unrestricted' is wrong, because the default top_k=50 applies.
- **Test output:** Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct: greedy procs=[rep_penalty=1.05] in both the sequential and batched paths; sample procs=[rep_penalty=1.05, top_k=20, top_p=0.8]. Qwen2.5-3B base: greedy procs=[]; sample procs=[top_k=50]. OLMo-2-1B base and Instruct: greedy procs=[]; sample procs=[top_k=50]. Tulu-3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: greedy procs=[]; sample procs=[top_k=50, top_p=0.9]. Same tiny weights with do_sample=False: responses differ = True ('\n\n\n\n\n defense...' vs '\n\n\n\n\n\n defense...'). Manifest: do_sample=False, temperature=1.0. Merged-setting keys in manifest or provenance: all False.
- **Test file:** [repro/F5_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F5_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F6. Completed-run guard skips keys absent from old manifests -> overwrite

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [extraction/generate_and_extract_roles.py:166](../../extraction/generate_and_extract_roles.py#L166)

- **Claim:** check_completed_run compares only the keys that the old manifest contains. The reference clouds were written before the guard existed, so they lack seed, temperature, chunk, batch_size, fewshot, stop_marker, revision and tokenizer_name. A run that differs only in those settings passes the guard and overwrites the completed cloud. The resume check also does not hash the record list, the FEWSHOT text, or the roles/questions source (ASSISTANT_AXIS_DIR), so stale shards can be reused under changed prompts.
- **Failure case:** `generate_and_extract_roles --n_questions 40 --seed 1 --batch_size 16 --fewshot --out_dir data/embeddings_roles_resp_40q` finds no mismatch, because model_name, n_questions, max_new_tokens, do_sample and n_records all match. It overwrites the 55,200-record reference cloud that verify_pilot uses, and the question sample is different. A second case: resume a partial run after setting ASSISTANT_AXIS_DIR to a live checkout. Earlier shards built from the vendored roles are merged with new ones.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I reran the test, and it runs the real main() and check_completed_run against a copy of the real data/embeddings_roles_resp_40q manifest. The A0 control refuses a max_new_tokens change. The run with --seed 1 --batch_size 16 --fewshot passes the guard and overwrites the sentinel cloud, and only 7 of the 40 questions are shared. A resume after an ASSISTANT_AXIS_DIR change, or after a FEWSHOT text change, merges 4 of 8 rows built from old prompts into the same cloud, and those rows carry the new metadata.
- **Test output:** Stored manifest keys: do_sample, hidden, max_new_tokens, model_name, n_layers, n_questions, n_records, n_roles, primary_layer, token_basis. Absent run_config keys: seed, temperature, chunk, batch_size, fewshot, stop_marker, revision, tokenizer_name. A0 control: rc=1, refused with {'max_new_tokens': (128, 64)}. A: rc=0, no refusal. Manifest after: seed=1, fewshot=True, batch_size=16, n_records=55200. The sentinel prompt_avg.npy changed shape from (3,2) to (55200,3,4), so it was overwritten. The seed 0 and seed 1 samples share only 7/40 question ids. The direct check_completed_run call passed …
- **Test file:** [repro/F6_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F6_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F7. Provenance stamp lost when sklearn absent; no model SHA or torch/transformers

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [src/manifold_persona/provenance.py:54](../../src/manifold_persona/provenance.py#L54)

- **Claim:** generate_and_extract_roles.py:331 calls write_stamp. run_stamp imports sklearn at the top, and on an extraction pod without sklearn the whole stamp is replaced by the error, which loses git commit, argv and env. When the stamp succeeds, it still records no torch or transformers version and no resolved model commit. The manifest stores `revision: null` (meaning 'main' at run time) instead of the snapshot SHA.
- **Failure case:** Published cloud qwen3b-base cannot be traced to a code commit, command line, model snapshot or library version. A later 'main' update to a model repo would silently change a re-run while the manifest stays identical.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. Real write_stamp/run_stamp replace the whole stamp with a stamp_error when sklearn is missing, because run_stamp imports sklearn unconditionally at provenance.py:54. Real main() then writes no git commit, argv or env, and the stored qwen3b-base provenance shows the same loss. When the stamp works, it has no torch or transformers version and no resolved model SHA, and the manifest writes revision=None.
- **Test output:** A: {'stamp_error': 'ModuleNotFoundError: import of sklearn halted; None in sys.modules'}. B: run_stamp keys = [argv, data, env, git_branch, git_commit, git_dirty, numpy, python, sklearn, timestamp], with no torch, transformers or revision. C1: manifest revision = None. The resolved SHA aa8e72537993ba99e69dfaafa59ed015b17504d1 is available from the HF config but is not in the manifest or provenance. C2: provenance = stamp_error only. D: the stored qwen3b-base provenance.json = {'stamp_error': "ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'sklearn'"}, and its manifest has no 'revision' key at all.
- **Test file:** [repro/F7_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F7_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F8. Batched left-padded generation on MPS gives non-finite garbage, no guard

◐ proven, narrower · ⚪ low · [src/manifold_persona/generate.py:157](../../src/manifold_persona/generate.py#L157)

- **Claim:** generate_and_extract_batched left-pads and generates on whatever device the caller passes. pick_device() picks MPS first. steering/run_steering.py records a measured MPS bug: a left-padded batch of 2 or more gives non-finite logits and emits token 0 ('!!!!'), at every dtype. generate.py has no isfinite check on logits or on the pooled activations, so it writes the garbage to the cloud.
- **Failure case:** On a Mac, `python -m extraction.generate_and_extract_roles --batch_size 8` loads on mps. The responses come out as '!!!!!!', and the hidden states of those tokens are pooled and saved as fp16, possibly NaN/inf. The shard merge checks only shapes, so a full cloud of meaningless points is finalized and the checkpoints are deleted. Only the later compaction step (depth_manifest 'nonfinite_values') would catch NaN, and it would not catch finite garbage.
- **Test result:** The finding is true in its core claim. The real batched extraction path and main() have no finiteness check. Non-finite activations and '!!!!' responses reach the final cloud, and the script deletes the checkpoints. The MPS left-padding trigger is still unproven here, because GPU use was forbidden and the test simulates the bug with NaN weights.
- **Test output:** pick_device() -> mps; load_model_and_tokenizer -> device mps, dtype float16. generate_and_extract_batched raised no exception; responses ['!!!!!!' x4]; avg non-finite 256/768 (layer 2 all NaN, layers 0-1 finite); last non-finite 256. _assert_finite_logits raises 'non-finite logits before generation (batch=2, seq=30, device=cpu)'. main(): error None; final prompt_avg.npy (4,3,64) has 256 non-finite values; _ckpt deleted True; manifest written; saved responses ['!!!!!' x4]; response_stats share_empty 0.0, share_looping 0.0.
- **Test file:** [repro/F8_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F8_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F9. Shift matrix has 21% duplicated rows; verdict ignores the null

✅ proven · ⚪ low · [diagnostics/03_experiment1_shift_rank.py:91](../../diagnostics/03_experiment1_shift_rank.py#L91)

- **Claim:** n_shared counts every position after the system block. The first 5 such positions ('<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>user\n') have the same causal prefix for every question, so their dh rows are exact copies repeated n_questions times. The SVD weights these near-system positions 10x, which biases SV1 share, gain CV and cos(v1). The GATED-CONSTANT verdict then uses an absolute 0.7 threshold and does not compare against the null that the script computes.
- **Failure case:** At the defaults (Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct, 10 questions), each role's matrix has 240 rows, and 50 of them are 10 copies of 5 vectors. If those large near-boundary shifts share a direction, SV1 rises and the script prints 'GATED-CONSTANT SUPPORTED' even when the shift on question tokens is high-rank.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. The test calls the real shift_matrix and build_matched_pair. They return 240 rows per role, and the first 5 shared positions ('<|im_end|>\n<|im_start|>user\n') are identical across all 10 questions, so about 20% of the rows are copies. So score_groups gave SE values of 0 where a fresh run gives 1.10 to 1.61. The claim that the manifest records the new settings comes from reading the code only; the test did not run that part.

### F17. 'Holds' test is weak on nested tiers and counts null metrics as holding

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [exploratory/per_persona/figures_qa_sweep.py:94](../../exploratory/per_persona/figures_qa_sweep.py#L94)

- **Claim:** The tiers are nested (q40 ⊂ q80 ⊂ ...), so the tier estimates share most of their data and are positively correlated. Overlap of two 95% CIs is also a much looser test than a difference test. Together these make `ci_overlaps_ref` almost always True. The sign-flip test uses point estimates. So a metric with r≈0 at every tier is reported 'holds' if its noise stays the same sign, and is reported 'MOVES' if the noise crosses zero. The 'N/M metrics hold' count therefore mixes replicated effects with replicated nulls, and the null cases are labelled at random.
- **Failure case:** A metric with r = 0.02, 0.03, 0.01, 0.02, 0.04 (CI ±0.12) gives holds=True and adds to 'metrics hold'. A metric with r = 0.01, -0.005, 0.008, 0.002, 0.004 gives sign_flip=True and is reported 'MOVES', but the two are the same null.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. On the failure-scenario inputs, the real verdicts() and main() report a same-sign null as "holds" (counted in "3/4 metrics hold"). They report a null that crosses zero as a sign flip ("MOVES"), and they also call a 0.20 shift in r "holds". The real question_tiers() confirms that the tiers are nested (q40 is 50% of q80).
- **Test output:** question_tiers nested: True; q40 share of q80: 0.50; q180 share of q240: 0.75. verdicts(): null_positive_noise r 0.01..0.04, sign_flip=False, overlap=True, holds=True. null_crosses_zero r -0.005..0.01, sign_flip=True, overlap=True, holds=False. real_effect r 0.39..0.42, holds=True. moved_by_0.2 r 0.10..0.30, overlap=True, holds=True. main() printed 'axis_proj: 3/4 metrics hold across tiers' and 'sign flips: ['null_crosses_zero']'.
- **Test file:** [repro/F17_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F17_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

### F18. cos_centroid is the same number as cos_axis, so 2 of 4 predictors are one

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [exploratory/per_persona/study_panel.py:84](../../exploratory/per_persona/study_panel.py#L84)

- **Claim:** In add_centroid_predictors, the roles are mean-centred first, so assistant_axis() returns unit(Xc[default]). This makes cos_axis = cos(Xc_r, Xc_default) = cos_centroid exactly, and axis_proj = mean_norm * cos_centroid. The ladder treats axis_proj and cos_centroid as two independent definitions of closeness, and controls for mean_norm. This leaves the two rungs nearly the same test.
- **Failure case:** Run study_panel, then the ladder with PREDICTORS=[axis_proj, cos_centroid, ...] and CTRL_ALL=[log_var, mean_norm]. A metric that 'holds under both axis_proj and cos_centroid' gets counted as two confirmations, but it is one finding. The families.py text says cos_centroid references default's centroid 'and axis_proj does not'. That is false, because the axis IS default's centred centroid.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. In add_centroid_predictors, the real code makes cos_axis exactly equal to cos_centroid, and axis_proj equals mean_norm*cos_centroid. So the families.py statement that axis_proj does not reference default's centroid is false. The claim about how much this overlap matters for the ladder is weaker on real data (partial r +0.785) than on this synthetic data (0.994).
- **Test output:** max|cos_axis - cos_centroid| = 3.96e-10. max|axis_proj - mean_norm*cos_centroid| = 5.79e-09. Raw Pearson(axis_proj, cos_centroid) = 0.9942 on synthetic data. ctrl_all partial r: axis_proj vs y = +0.1364, cos_centroid vs y = +0.1339. Partial r(axis_proj, cos_centroid | log_var, mean_norm) = +0.9943. The families text claim that axis_proj does not reference default's centroid is present = True.

### F22. Planted-manifold calibration is noise-free and 'scale' is a no-op

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [exploratory/per_persona/compute_budget.py:64](../../exploratory/per_persona/compute_budget.py#L64)

- **Claim:** `planted_manifold` claims to match the data's spectrum and noise floor. It does neither. `scale[: X.shape[1]].mean()` is one scalar, and the ID estimators are scale-invariant, so it changes nothing. The added noise is 1% of mean |X|, which is ~6e-5 of the signal variance. Part B also runs `id_estimates` on the raw 2048-d points, but the panel (metrics.py) runs it on PCA-50. So the 'N needed' result and the GPU-hour budget come from a near-noiseless operation that the real data never gets.
- **Failure case:** Real role clouds have 17.7% residual variance, and calib_estimators reports that the noise floor inflates TwoNN from d=3 to 8-11 on raw points. In the planted clouds, the planted dimension is recovered at a much smaller N. So `target_points_per_role`, `questions_per_role_needed` and the Part C hours (quoted in REPORT.md by run_id_stage) are too low. For the 240q cloud, the full 331200x2048 float64 SVD only feeds this no-op scalar and can exhaust laptop RAM.
- **Test result:** The core finding is true, and the evidence comes from the real repo code. planted_manifold multiplies X by one scalar, spec[:ambient].mean() (the ratio is constant at 87.67), and the ID estimates are the same with or without it. Its noise is about 6e-5 of the variance. main()'s id_recovery_vs_n is the same (to 1e-14) for unit clouds and for anisotropic clouds scaled 1000x. Part B runs id_estimates on the raw 256-d points, but panel_metrics runs it on PCA-50. The test does not prove the claim that the budget is "too low".
- **Test output:** Part 1 (ambient=512, geomspace spectrum 1e3..1e-2): the ratio Xs/X1 is a constant 87.668622, which equals spec[:ambient].mean(). id_estimates gives TwoNN 4.947047 vs 4.947047, MLE 5.538604 vs 5.538604 and lPCA 10 vs 10, so the results are identical. The output variance does not follow the spectrum: the top1/top10 eigenvalue ratio is 11.6, but the spectrum ratio is 1.5. Part 2: the variance outside the rank-2d signal subspace is 5.90e-05 (d=5), 6.15e-05 (d=10) and 6.19e-05 (d=15). ### F25. Between-role baseline uses same-question halves; within uses disjoint

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [exploratory/per_persona/residual_noise_null.py:89](../../exploratory/per_persona/residual_noise_null.py#L89)

- **Claim:** Within-role overlap compares half A (even questions) with half B (odd questions). The between-role baseline compares role i-1 half A with role i half A, which is the SAME question set. Residual structure tied to a question and shared across roles therefore raises 'between' but not 'within'. This biases the margin and confounds decision rule 2 (L10 vs L19 margin), because the amount of such shared structure can change with depth. Adjacent between-pairs also share a role, but the bootstrap resamples them as iid, so the CIs are too narrow.
- **Failure case:** Simulation with the script's own half_residual/topk/overlap: 60 roles, a 5x40 grid, no role-specific structure, plus question-specific noise directions shared across roles. Result: within=0.037, between=0.476, margin=-0.44. With a real role-specific 5-dim structure added, the margin is +0.33, shrunk by the same bias. A layer with more shared question-specific variance gets a lower margin with no change in role structure, so rule 2 can flip.
- **Test result:** The main claim is true and comes from the real code. main() line 90 compares prev_Va with Va, which are both even-question halves, while the within-role overlap uses even vs odd halves. When the real main() runs with only shared question-tied noise, the margin CI is below 0 ([-0.040,-0.022] at qnoise=3; -0.32 at qnoise=8). A disjoint-half control, built with the repo's own functions, stays at the iid level (~0.155), and the pure-noise null gives a margin of about 0. The noise also shrinks a real role margin and can reverse decision rule 2 between L10 and L19. The secondary claim (bootstrap CIs too narrow) was not reproduced: the ratio was 0.96.
- **Test output:** Data: 276 roles, 1 distinct question set, 40 q/role. A0 (pure iid noise): margin=0.004, CI [-0.002, 0.012], which is unbiased. A (shared q-noise 3, no role structure): within=0.158, between=0.189, margin=-0.029, CI [-0.040, -0.022]. The disjoint-half control gave between=0.153. A8 (q-noise 8): within=0.155, between=0.473, margin=-0.320, CI [-0.330, -0.296]. The disjoint control gave 0.156. B (same role structure): margin was 0.230 [0.222, 0.239] without q-noise and 0.192 [0.179, 0.197] with it. C (identical role structure; q-noise is 3 at L10 and 0.5 at L19): L10 margin=0.196 [0.190, 0.204] …

The same metric divided by cloud_diameter gives -0.42, and H1_total_persistence gives -0.17 vs -0.22. compare_stages reads the low dflt_pct of H0_total_persistence as 'tight + low-dim = privileged', but that reading is partly default's cloud size.
- **Test result:** Mostly true, with one part false. I ran the real metrics.panel_metrics. When the cloud is scaled by 3, the H0/H1/H2 lifetime columns grow by exactly 3x, and the density and curvature columns do not change. topology.betti_from_diagrams sets the infinite H0 bar to the diameter. On the stored RLVR panel, real compare_stages.stage_stats gives r(H0_TP, rms_radius) = +0.76, and r_ctrl moves from -0.31 to -0.42 after dividing by diameter (H1: -0.17 to -0.22), which matches the claim. The H0-entropy part of the claim is false: the real code shows entropy does not change under pure rescaling.

For an OLMo parent run with MP_MODEL_NAME unset, all sys_tok values use the Qwen BPE.
- **Test result:** I re-ran the test and confirmed the main defect with real repo code. system_prompt_stats counts the default role's empty (NaN) system prompt as 'nan' (1 token), so the mean comes out at 6.2 tokens. The downstream harm is smaller than the finding says: the printed percentile and the "default is shortest" statement stay correct (0% either way), and the tokenizer mismatch changes no token counts on the stored data.

### F52. Positive control gates on role-shuffle null; covgauss decider has no power

◐ proven, narrower · 🟠 high · [manifold/run.py:86](../../manifold/run.py#L86)

- **Claim:** The run checks its positive control against the role-shuffle permutation null, but the H1 verdict comes from covgauss_null. On a planted, curved 3-D manifold, covgauss_null cannot tell real from null, so the control passes and still says nothing about the test that decides.
- **Failure case:** Run `python -m manifold.run`. positive_control() returns PASS, because the synthetic manifold beats the role-shuffle null (p=0.02, rel_red=0.52). The decider then compares C_role with covgauss_null. That null gives the same R2 even when a true curved manifold is present. REPORT.md shows 'Positive control PASS', then 'H1 NOT SUPPORTED ... does not beat the covgauss null'. A reader takes this as an interpretable negative. In fact the deciding test always returns about p~0.45, whatever the truth is.
- **Test result:** The finding is true at the data-matched calibration. The real positive_control passes against the role-shuffle null (p=0.0196, rel_red=0.42). The real covgauss_null, which decides the verdict, cannot tell the planted curved k=3 manifold apart. It gives R2 0.647 vs null median 0.644, p=0.484, rel_red=0.007, and verdict 'null'. On a flat Gaussian cloud it gives about the same result (p=0.548). So the control does not show that the deciding test has power, and the real report code still prints "PASS" and then "H1 NOT SUPPORTED".
- **Test output:** calibration noise/dim=1.252, role-spread=11.326 (from the stored run log). Positive control against the role-shuffle null: R2=0.647, null_med=0.388, p=0.0196, rel_red=0.42, PASS. This matches the stored run 2026-08-03T13-41. Planted curved manifold against covgauss: R2=0.647, null_med=0.644, p=0.484, rel_red=0.007, verdict 'null'. Flat Gaussian cloud against covgauss: R2=0.648, null_med=0.649, p=0.548, rel_red=-0.005, verdict 'null'. Report output: '| **Positive** ... p=0.0196 | ✅ PASS |', then 'H1 NOT SUPPORTED', then 'The fit **does not beat the covgauss (curvature-only) null** (p=0.484)...'

### F55. Decision rule ignores failed positive controls for WORSE/FLAT verdicts

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [manifold/sweep.py:254](../../manifold/sweep.py#L254)

- **Claim:** The positive-control gate only applies to the 'better' branch. The 'worse' branch (RR(10) <= RR(276)-band) and the RR(276) baseline never check pc_pass, and the rr frame includes failed-control cells. A failed control at n=10 or n=25 therefore pushes the verdict toward 'hypothesis supported'. The report then states that failed cells are 'excluded from the decision rule', which the code does not do.
- **Failure case:** The n=10 positive control fails because the pipeline cannot detect a manifold at that n, so the real RR(10) is also low (for example 0.20 vs RR(276)=0.45). `worse` is True and the run records 'small-n WORSE (hypothesis supported)'. The verdict rests on a cell the plan says must not be interpreted. In the same way, a clear small-n improvement with a failed control at n=25 becomes FLAT ('hypothesis supported') and not 'excluded/uninterpretable'.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. In my re-run, the real sweep.main gave "small-n WORSE (hypothesis supported)" when every n=10 positive control failed. It gave "FLAT (hypothesis supported)" when the n=25 controls failed and the improvement was clear. The REPORT.md from the same run says failed cells are "excluded from the decision rule". The code shows why: only the `better` branch checks pc_pass, and both `worse` and the rr frame include the failed cells.
- **Test output:** Run A: decision='small-n WORSE (hypothesis supported)'. posctrl_failed_cells=all 5 n=10 seeds. small['10'].mean=0.202. rr_by_n keys=['10','25','276']. REPORT has 'excluded from the decision rule': True. Run B: decision='FLAT (hypothesis supported: legibility, not evidence)'. RR(10)=RR(25)=0.802 against RR(276)=0.451. posctrl_failed_cells=all 5 n=25 seeds. REPORT claims exclusion: True. Control run C (all controls pass, same RR values as A): decision='small-n WORSE (hypothesis supported)', the same verdict as A. So a failed control does not change the verdict at all.
- **Test file:** [repro/F55_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F55_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

- **Claim:** The post-hoc share is computed as abs(d_pc / d_rr). When the role-free positive control moves in the opposite direction to the real decider, the report still says that the control 'reproduces' N% of the decider's change. The share can also exceed 100% with no warning.
- **Failure case:** The real RR rises by +0.10 from n=276 to n=10 (d_rr=+0.10). The control's RR falls by 0.10 (d_pc=-0.10). The report prints '**100% of the decider's own change across the sweep** is reproduced by data with no role content', and §6.3 repeats it. The actual result is the reverse: the control moves against the decider, so none of the change is explained by SNR.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I re-ran the test, and the real build_sweep_report printed "100% of the decider's own change ... is reproduced" when the control moved opposite to the decider (d_rr=+0.10, d_pc=-0.10). §6.3 repeats that line. The report also printed 300% with no warning when the control moved 3x more. This is because line 435 uses abs(d_pc/d_rr).
- **Test output:** Case A: d_rr=+0.10, d_pc=-0.10, signed ratio -1.00. Section 6.1 prints: "changes by -0.10 ... versus 0.10 for the real roles — **100% of the decider's own change across the sweep** is reproduced by data with no role content". Section 6.3 repeats "**100% of the decider's own change". Case B: d_rr=+0.10, d_pc=+0.30. The report prints "**300% of the decider's own change across the sweep** is reproduced" with no warning. Case C (sanity check): the report prints 50%.

### F59. k=2 TPS kernel is log r, not the documented r^2 log r

✅ proven · ⚪ low · [manifold/tps.py:36](../../manifold/tps.py#L36)

- **Claim:** thin_plate_kernel computes r^(dim-2)*log r. For dim=2 that is log r, which is singular at the anchors. The docstring says dim==2 -> r^2 log r. The k=2 robustness fit therefore uses a different and spiky surface: decode diverges as u approaches each anchor, then snaps back to the anchor at r<1e-10.
- **Failure case:** run.py:225-226 runs the 'C_role[k=2]' robustness and its null. Each fitted surface has log singularities at every anchor. Near anchor 0, ||decode(u)-anchor|| = 72, 124, 200 at offsets 1e-3, 1e-5, 1e-8, then 2.6e-10 at 1e-11. The reported k=2 R2 and its Holm-adjusted p come from that object, not from a thin-plate spline. On a 120-role control, R2 = 0.7408 with the code kernel and 0.7488 with r^2 log r.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. The real manifold.tps.thin_plate_kernel(r, 2) returns exactly log r, not r^2 log r. A real k=2 fit_manifold surface on a 120-role positive control moves away from anchor 0 as the query gets closer: 22, 32 and 47 at offsets 1e-3, 1e-5 and 1e-8, against an RMS spread of 11.3. It then snaps back to the anchor below r=1e-10, while the standard kernel gives distances near the offset. The C_role[k=2] R2 also changes, from 0.7408 to 0.7488. The effect on R2 is small, which fits the low severity.
- **Test output:** thin_plate_kernel(r, dim=2) at r=[1e-12, 1e-8, 1e-3, 0.1, 0.5, 2] returned [0, -18.42, -6.91, -2.30, -0.69, 0.69], which matches log r exactly. The r^2 log r values are [0, -1.8e-15, -6.9e-6, -0.023, -0.173, 2.77]. For a k=2 manifold (D=50, role-mean RMS spread 11.28), ||decode(u) - anchor0|| was 22.31, 32.34 and 47.4 at offsets 1e-3, 1e-5 and 1e-8, then 1.8e-10 at 1e-11. With the r^2 log r kernel the same distances were 1.7e-3, 1.7e-5, 1.7e-8 and 3.7e-11, so the surface is smooth there. ### F61. C_raw 'CV' assigns folds per point, so same-role points leak

✅ proven · ⚪ low · [manifold/pipeline.py:175](../../manifold/pipeline.py#L175)

- **Claim:** construction_C_raw assigns each raw point to a random fold. Every test point's same-role siblings (24 others: same role, other instructions and questions) mostly stay in train and can be TPS anchors, which the surface interpolates exactly. The reported CV R2 therefore measures interpolation within seen roles, not generalisation to held-out roles.
- **Failure case:** With 25 points per role and a 600-anchor cap from about 5,500 training points, each test point has about 2 same-role anchors on average. The surface passes through them, so test residuals are smaller than for an unseen role. REPORT/metrics.csv list 'C_raw R2 (CV)' as held-out evidence, but it is optimistic compared with a role-grouped split.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. The real construction_C_raw (pipeline.py:175) assigns folds per point. With a role structure, it reports CV R2 = 0.809, but a role-grouped split that uses the same repo fit and score gives 0.346. In the no-role control, the two splits agree (0.376 and 0.376), so the gap comes from same-role leakage. The test does not measure how large the effect is on real data.
- **Test output:** Role-structured cloud (between-role sd 1.0, within-role sd 0.3, D=20, 276 roles x 25): construction_C_raw R2 = 0.809, role-grouped R2 = 0.346, gap 0.463. Test points whose role has at least one training anchor: 0.86-0.91 per fold. Mean same-role anchors per test point: 2.07-2.15, close to the predicted 600/276 = 2.2. No-role control: per-point R2 = 0.376, grouped R2 = 0.376, gap 0.001.
- **Test file:** [repro/F61_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F61_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

The one-line answer in the same report says the decider "neither falsifies nor confirms the hypothesis". For WORSE, the JSON says "hypothesis supported", but the one-line answer says only "not falsified". The cause is the decision string literals at manifold/sweep.py:259-261.

One-line answer: 'Coarsening the role set lowers the measured effect size; the hypothesis is not falsified by this run.' Both contradictions = True.

### F69. MP_STEER_AXIS_FILE override axis sign is never checked

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [steering/geometry.py:340](../../steering/geometry.py#L340)

- **Claim:** The override replaces the mean-difference axis with any unit vector from a .npy file. The code does not check the vector's sign against the Assistant direction, for example that the default rows project higher than the role rows. The whole module assumes 'higher axis_proj = more Assistant-like': near50 = top of argsort(-axis_proj), and the linear arm's alpha sign follows the same rule.
- **Failure case:** A logistic-regression probe trained with label 1 = role-play (weights point away from default) is passed via MP_STEER_AXIS_FILE for the base-vs-RLVR causal test. near50 then holds the most role-like roles and far50 the most Assistant-like roles. The rank pairing and the direction of every linear-arm dose are inverted, but the figures are still labelled 'toward/away from Assistant'. Nothing fails.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. When the override axis points away from default, the real load_geometry accepts it with no error or warning. It then swaps near50 and far50 (50/50 overlap with the baseline far50), and alpha=+1 in linear_axis_vector moves the state away from the Assistant. The impact depends on someone passing a probe with the opposite sign. No current repo file does this.
- **Test output:** Baseline: cos(axis, d)=1.000; near50 mean assistantness=+1.765; far50=-1.764; alpha=+1 delta·d=+1.574. ### F70. DeltaStats mostly counts pad tokens fed after EOS

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [steering/run_steering.py:404](../../steering/run_steering.py#L404)

- **Claim:** The dynamic hook records DeltaStats (overshoot, delta_norm_mean, frac_out_of_knots) at every position the layer sees. In batched greedy generate, a row that has finished keeps getting forward passes on pad tokens until the longest row stops, and left-pad prefill positions are also counted. The WP5 numbers then describe pad-token activations more than real response tokens.
- **Failure case:** CUDA batch of 20 (default_batch_size), GEN_CEILING=1024. One steered row loops to 1024 tokens and the other 19 stop at about 50. Then about 19*974 = 18.5k post-EOS pad positions go through make_manifold_axis_delta_fn / make_arm2_delta_fn, versus about 1.3k real positions. The reported frac_out_of_knots and overshoot_mean come mostly from pad embeddings. This is the check that the manifold arm did not become linear, so its result is wrong without any warning.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. The real generate_cell runs the manifold_axis dynamic hook, and its real DeltaStats report shows n_coords=1520. That is every position, including 192 left-pad prefill positions and 228 post-EOS pad positions. Pads change the result: frac_out_of_knots is 0.26 as reported, 0.16 on real tokens only, and 0.52 on pads only. The word "mostly" is true only when a few rows run far longer than the others.
- **Test output:** B=20, padded prompt L=37, T=40. Per-row stop: 7 rows stop at step 1, 13 rows run to 40. Left-pad prefill positions = 192. Post-EOS pad positions fed = 228. Real positions = 1100. DeltaStats n_coords = n_positions = 1520 = B*L + B*(T-1), which is every position including pads. Reported frac_out_of_knots = 0.2605. Over real positions only = 0.1609. Over pad positions only = 0.5214. Pads are 27.6% of the counted positions. In an earlier variant with a wider knot range, 18/20 rows stopped at step 1: counted 1520 vs 644 real (57.6% pad).
- **Test file:** [repro/F70_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F70_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [steering/capture_activations.py:86](../../steering/capture_activations.py#L86)

- **Claim:** The module docstring says the replay gives 'the same quantity, layer and token basis the role cloud is built from'. It does not. The cloud mean includes the generated EOS token and covers at most 128 response tokens. The replay re-encodes response text decoded with skip_special_tokens=True, so the EOS is gone. It also pools up to max_len(768) minus prompt tokens of a response that can be 1024 tokens long, and silently truncates the rest.
- **Failure case:** Short introspective answer (about 15 tokens) from a Qwen run: the cloud centroid weights the <|im_end|> activation at about 1/16, and the replay drops it. That gives a systematic offset between every replayed point and the centroids. A steered 900-token response is pooled over about 700 tokens and then cut at 768 without any count. The cloud never saw more than 128 tokens. Mean position drifts with length, so 'where steering landed' in the shared PCA mixes in a length artefact and an EOS-exclusion artefact.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. I ran the test myself. The real generate_and_extract cloud mean includes the generated <|im_end|> (rel err 2e-4 against the mean that includes EOS). The real capture_activations replay matches the mean without EOS (rel err 2e-4). The two vectors differ by 27% for the same text. In the second case, the replay pools only the first 742 of 1035 response tokens at --max-len 768, differs by 18% from the replay of the full response, and prints no truncation count. Each BUG decision comes from the output of the real repo code.
- **Test output:** cloud R=7 tokens, last token id 151645 (<|im_end|>) = EOS; decoded response has no <|im_end|>. Long response: prompt 26 tokens, response 1035 tokens after re-encode, 742 tokens pooled at max-len 768; replay768 matches mean(first 742) (rel diff 2.2e-4); replay768 vs full replay rel diff 0.18. ### F72. Empty-response rows saved as all-zero vectors

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [steering/capture_activations.py:99](../../steering/capture_activations.py#L99)

- **Claim:** When a row has no response tokens, the code skips it with `continue` and leaves `out[s0+bi]` at its np.zeros initial value. The .npy and .meta.parquet still have that row, and nothing marks it. Downstream PCA and distance code then treats a vector at the origin as a real landing point.
- **Failure case:** At extreme alpha a steered model emits EOS at once, so the response is ''. For that row plen equals the sequence length, start >= h.shape[1], and the row stays at 0. With residual norms in the tens to hundreds, the zero rows become far outliers that pull the PCA axes and give fake 'collapse' distances. The only record is a printed count. The cloud's own extractor uses the last prompt token for this case, so the two paths also disagree.
- **Test result:** True for the core defect. The real steering/capture_activations.main() wrote row 2 (empty response) to the .npy as an exact zero vector. The other rows are non-zero, and the meta parquet keeps that row with no column that marks it. The only record is the printed "1 rows with no response tokens" count. The sub-claim that the cloud extractor uses the last prompt token is wrong in detail, but the two paths do disagree.
- **Test output:** capture_activations printed "wrote ... acts.npy ((4, 64)) | 1 rows with no response tokens". Row norms were [0.098, 0.084, 0.0, 0.106]. Row 2 (empty response) is all-zero = True. npy rows = meta rows = 4. meta columns = ['arm','alpha','role','judge_score'], with no empty marker. Cloud extractor on an immediate-EOS tiny model: generate_and_extract gave response='', norm 0.239, non-zero, equal to neither the last prompt token nor a single EOS token (it averages the generated EOS tokens). generate_and_extract_batched gave response='', norm 0.237, equal to the EOS-token hidden state.

### F73. Completeness gate skipped when --n-roles is narrower than the recorded scope

✅ proven · 🟡 medium · [steering/run_steering.py:669](../../steering/run_steering.py#L669)

- **Claim:** all_cells is rebuilt from the widened recorded scope only when the arm set changed. When only n_roles differs, all_cells stays the narrower grid of this call. The gate then passes and the aggregation globs every shard on disk. The partial artifact is written with partial=false.
- **Failure case:** A full run (n_roles=None) runs as 4 shards, and some workers are killed. Someone then runs `--n-roles 5` on the same dir as a preview. scope['n_roles'] becomes None, scope['arms'] == sorted(arms), so no rebuild happens. all_cells holds only the 5-role cells, which all exist, so missing=[]. generations_L19.parquet is written from whatever shards exist, and generation_rate.json says cells_expected=<5-role count>, cells_missing=0, partial=false. This is the silent partial that the gate comment says cannot happen.
- **Test result:** The finding is true. The test calls the real main() and build_grid(). After a full-scope run, a `--n-roles 5` run in the same dir keeps the recorded n_roles=None. The arm set is the same, so main() does not rebuild all_cells (run_steering.py:669 checks only arms). The real code then writes generations_L19.parquet from 380 of 950 cells and records cells_expected=95, cells_missing=0, partial=false. This happens with --collect and also in a normal preview run.
- **Test output:** For both variants: recorded n_roles before=None, after=None; arms=[linear_axis, manifold_axis, unsteered]. Full-scope cells=950, present=380, truly missing=570. generations_L19.parquet has 7600 rows (380 shards x 20). generation_rate.json has cells_expected=95, cells_missing=0, partial=False. Expected result: no artifact (the gate refuses), or cells_expected=950, cells_missing=570, partial=True.

### F76. judge_concurrent never retries failed rows; unjudged rows drop non-randomly

◐ proven, narrower · 🟡 medium · [steering/judge_concurrent.py:107](../../steering/judge_concurrent.py#L107)

- **Claim:** Rows whose call failed (ERROR_*, BAD_JSON, NOT_AN_OBJECT, OUT_OF_SCHEMA, NO_TEXT_BLOCK) are written to the checkpoint with judge_score=None and counted as done on resume (`if k not in done`). They are never re-judged. parse_verdict also never raises, so a refusal or truncated reply is not retried inside judge_one either. persona_judge.py and rolefilter.py both retry None rows, and persona_judge says 'Same fix as judge.py', but this module lacks that fix.
- **Failure case:** A degenerate high-|alpha| manifold-arm response makes the judge refuse or emit out-of-schema JSON. That row is stored as None and stays None after every resume. figures_steering.make_figures excludes None rows 'from both numerator and denominator', so per-(arm, alpha) category fractions are computed on a subset that is missing the hardest cases. These are mostly nonsensical/weird_role responses in strongly steered cells. The bias falls on the linear-vs-manifold comparison and does not go away on re-run.
- **Test result:** The finding is true for the code as written. judge_concurrent.py:107 counts every checkpointed key as done, so rows that failed with BAD_JSON, OUT_OF_SCHEMA or ERROR_* are never sent again on resume. parse_verdict failures are not retried inside judge_one, and _role_fraction_matrix then drops those rows from the denominator. How large the bias is on the real steering data is not shown.
- **Test output:** Run 1: 6 rows, failed=3 (BAD_JSON, OUT_OF_SCHEMA 'refused', ERROR_RuntimeError). Run 2 (resume, client always OK): "rows=6 already_done=6 todo=0". The 3 failed rows got 0 calls in run 2 and stay NaN. judge_one made 1 call each for BADJSON and REFUSE, and 4 calls for RAISE. With the failed rows set to truly 'nonsensical', the figure fraction for nonsensical was 0.333 against a true value of 0.667.
- **Test file:** [repro/F76_test.py](../../output/code_review_2026-10-08/repro/F76_test.py) · my re-run: BUG_REPRODUCED
